Summary

The farm bill is an omnibus, multiyear law and the primary piece of legislation that governs an array of agricultural and food programs. The most recently enacted farm bill is the Agriculture Improvement Act of 2018 (2018 farm bill; [P.L. 115-334](http://www.congress.gov/cgi-lis/bdquery/R?d115:FLD002:@1(115+334))). On April 30, 2026, the House passed the Farm, Food, and National Security Act of 2026 ([H.R. 7567](http://www.congress.gov/cgi-lis/bdquery/z?d119:H.R.7567:)) to add, amend, and reauthorize some of the programs in the 2018 farm bill. On September 16, 2026, the Senate Committee on Agriculture, Nutrition, and Forestry reported the Agriculture Act of 2026 ([S. 5526](http://www.congress.gov/bill/119/s/5526)). This report provides an overview of [H.R. 7567](http://www.congress.gov/cgi-lis/bdquery/z?d119:H.R.7567:) and [S. 5526](http://www.congress.gov/bill/119/s/5526) and compares those bills with current law.

Across Titles I-XII, [H.R. 7567](http://www.congress.gov/cgi-lis/bdquery/z?d119:H.R.7567:) and [S. 5526](http://www.congress.gov/bill/119/s/5526) would reauthorize and amend food and agricultural policies in a variety of ways, with certain differences, as in the following examples. *Title I* of [H.R. 7567](http://www.congress.gov/cgi-lis/bdquery/z?d119:H.R.7567:) would restore tobacco eligibility for funding from the Commodity Credit Corporation (CCC), whereas [S. 5526](http://www.congress.gov/bill/119/s/5526) would amend and add reporting requirements of CCC expenditures and activities. *Title II* of both bills contains reauthorizations, amendments, and new programs that aim to incentivize farmers and ranchers to voluntarily implement resource-conserving practices on private land. Under [H.R. 7567](http://www.congress.gov/cgi-lis/bdquery/z?d119:H.R.7567:), proposed changes center on directing programs to specific resource concerns and production methods. [S. 5526](http://www.congress.gov/bill/119/s/5526) includes several changes relating to disaster and watershed programs, as well as guidelines for conservation practice standards. *Title III* of both bills would reauthorize and amend international food assistance and export programs. [H.R. 7567](http://www.congress.gov/bill/119/hr/7567) would move the responsibilities of the U.S. Agency for International Development (USAID) under the Food for Peace Act (P.L. 83-480, as amended) to the U.S. Department of Agriculture (USDA), including administration of Food for Peace Title II Grants. *Title IV* of both bills would generally extend the Supplemental Nutrition Assistance Program (SNAP) and other related nutrition programs through September 30, 2031. While both nutrition titles address many of the same nutrition policies, at times with substantive differences, there are provisions that are only in [H.R. 7567](http://www.congress.gov/cgi-lis/bdquery/z?d119:H.R.7567:) or in [S. 5526](http://www.congress.gov/bill/119/s/5526). Examples of policies only in [H.R. 7567](http://www.congress.gov/cgi-lis/bdquery/z?d119:H.R.7567:) are the amendment of the statutory purpose of SNAP to reflect health objectives and the mandate of a Government Accountability Office report on SNAP administrative expenses. Only in [S. 5526](http://www.congress.gov/bill/119/s/5526) are policies such as a one-year delay to states' SNAP benefit cost-sharing requirements, tribal input and supply chain response requirements in the Food Distribution Program on Indian Reservations (FDPIR) and Commodity Supplemental Food Program (CSFP), and stricter authorization rules for certain SNAP retailer types. *Title V* of both bills would increase the maximum loan amounts for farmers and ranchers who borrow from USDA. Both bills would add eligibility for farm loans to commercial fishing entities—the House-passed bill would allow farm ownership and farm operating loans and include fish processing facilities, and the Senate committee-reported bill would allow farm operating loans only and exclude fish processing. *Title VI* of both bills would expand the types of health care institutions eligible to refinance debt using Rural Development loans under certain circumstances. Only in [H.R. 7567](http://www.congress.gov/cgi-lis/bdquery/z?d119:H.R.7567:) would the Circuit Rider Program be expanded to provide rural water and wastewater systems with disaster recovery assistance. *Title VII* of both bills would reauthorize USDA agricultural research, extension, education, veterinary, and land-grant institution authorities through FY2031. [H.R. 7567](http://www.congress.gov/bill/119/hr/7567) would generally make broader administrative and programmatic changes, including repealing several existing authorities and establishing programs. [S. 5526](http://www.congress.gov/bill/119/s/5526) would generally retain more existing authorities, authorize higher funding levels for selected programs, and establish a smaller number of new initiatives than the House-passed bill. *Title VIII* of both bills has provisions relating to forestry research, federal forest management, and financial and technical assistance to nonfederal forest landowners. The House-passed bill includes a subtitle concerning giant sequoia protection, whereas the Senate committee-reported bill does not. *Title IX* of [H.R. 7567](http://www.congress.gov/bill/119/hr/7567) would reauthorize most of the 2018 farm bill energy title programs and repeal two programs, whereas the energy title of the Senate committee-reported bill would reauthorize all the energy title programs. Both bills would modify certain programs. The House-passed bill would add new sections to Title IX pertaining to solar energy; the Senate committee-reported bill would not. *Title* *X* of both bills would reauthorize USDA to issue block grants to states through FY2031 to enhance the competitiveness of specialty crops. Only in [H.R. 7567](http://www.congress.gov/cgi-lis/bdquery/z?d119:H.R.7567:) would the domestic hemp production program be amended to reflect changes to the statutory definition of *hemp* that were made in [P.L. 119-37](http://www.congress.gov/cgi-lis/bdquery/R?d119:FLD002:@1(119+37)). *Title XI* of both bills would modify the definition of *veteran farmers and ranchers* used in the Federal Crop Insurance Program and increase premium subsidies available for these individuals. Title XI of [H.R. 7567](http://www.congress.gov/bill/119/hr/7567) and [S. 5526](http://www.congress.gov/bill/119/s/5526) differ in terms of the changes they would make to final agency determinations, the composition of the board of the Federal Crop Insurance Corporation, and research and development priority areas. *Title XII* of [H.R. 7567](http://www.congress.gov/bill/119/hr/7567) would restrict a state from enacting and enforcing production standards on livestock products not produced in the state and amend the authorities of USDA's Office of Tribal Relations and the National Appeals Division. [S. 5526](http://www.congress.gov/bill/119/s/5526) would direct USDA to produce a report on fertilizer production and use.

---

Introduction

Congress has established federal policy relating to the food and agriculture sector through periodic farm bills since the 1930s. The farm bill is an omnibus, multiyear law and the primary piece of legislation governing an array of agricultural and food programs. Historically, policy areas addressed in farm bills provided support for certain commodities; this has expanded to support of wide-ranging programs and policies, such as commodities, conservation, trade, nutrition assistance, credit, rural development, research, forestry, energy, horticulture, and crop insurance.[1](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R48918.html#fn1 "CRS In Focus IF12047, Farm Bill Primer: Background and Status , by Jim Monke and Megan Stubbs .")

The farm bill contains numerous different authorities for programs to exist, operate, and receive funding. Certain programs are permanently authorized and would continue in the absence of new farm legislation. Other farm bill programs have authorizations that expire approximately every five years and require reauthorization to continue. The most recent farm bill, the Agriculture Improvement Act of 2018 (2018 farm bill; [P.L. 115-334](http://www.congress.gov/cgi-lis/bdquery/R?d115:FLD002:@1(115+334))), expired in 2023. It was extended three times, for a year at a time: in November 2023 to cover FY2024 and crop year 2024 ([P.L. 118-22](http://www.congress.gov/cgi-lis/bdquery/R?d118:FLD002:@1(118+22)), Division B, §102); in December 2024 to cover FY2025 and crop year 2025 ([P.L. 118-158](http://www.congress.gov/cgi-lis/bdquery/R?d118:FLD002:@1(118+158)), Division D, §4101); and in November 2025 to cover FY2026 ([P.L. 119-37](http://www.congress.gov/cgi-lis/bdquery/R?d119:FLD002:@1(119+37)), Division E, §5002).

Congress amended selected provisions of the 2018 farm bill through Title I of the FY2025 budget reconciliation law ([P.L. 119-21](http://www.congress.gov/cgi-lis/bdquery/R?d119:FLD002:@1(119+21))). The FY2025 budget reconciliation law did not reauthorize all expired or expiring programs or authorizations of the 2018 farm bill.[2](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R48918.html#fn2 "CRS Report R48775, The Farm Bill After FY2025 Budget Reconciliation: Frequently Asked Questions , by Jim Monke and Megan Stubbs .") For mandatory spending programs, budget reconciliation rules did not allow policy changes that did not have a budgetary effect. Policy changes and reauthorizations to discretionary spending programs are not allowed under budget reconciliation. The FY2025 budget reconciliation law included changes for mandatory spending programs in certain titles, including commodities, nutrition, crop insurance, and conservation, as well as relatively smaller programs with mandatory funding in the trade, research, energy, horticulture, and miscellaneous titles.

House Action

The Farm, Food, and National Security Act of 2026 ([H.R. 7567](http://www.congress.gov/cgi-lis/bdquery/z?d119:H.R.7567:)) would add to, amend, and reauthorize some of the programs in the 2018 farm bill. [H.R. 7567](http://www.congress.gov/cgi-lis/bdquery/z?d119:H.R.7567:) was introduced on February 13, 2026. The House Committee on Agriculture considered the bill and ordered it reported favorably, as amended, to the House on March 5, 2026, by a vote of 34-17. Members submitted 155 committee amendments. In committee markup,

* 45 amendments passed by vote (including 1 manager's amendment, 5 as part of an en bloc amendment, and 1 second-degree amendment to another amendment);[3](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R48918.html#fn3 "U.S. Congress, House Committee on Agriculture , \" Markup of 'To Consider H.R. 7567 , t he Farm, Food, and National Security Act of 202 6 , ' \" March 3-4, 2026, https://docs.house.gov/Committee/Calendar/ByEvent.aspx?EventID=118990 .")
* 29 amendments failed by vote (3 amendments failed by voice vote, and 26 amendments failed by recorded vote);
* 32 amendments were offered and withdrawn after discussion;
* 47 amendments were not offered; and
* 2 amendments were ruled out of order as not germane.

H.R. 7567 was reported on April 21, 2026, with [H.Rept. 119-620](http://www.congress.gov/cgi-lis/cpquery/R?cp119:FLD010:@1(hr620):). In developing the rule for floor consideration, 371 amendments were submitted, of which 57 were made in order for floor consideration ([H.Res. 1224](http://www.congress.gov/cgi-lis/bdquery/z?d119:H.Res.1224:), [H.Rept. 119-628](http://www.congress.gov/cgi-lis/cpquery/R?cp119:FLD010:@1(hr628):)).[4](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R48918.html#fn4 "House Committee on Rules, \" H.R. 7567—Farm, Food, and National Security Act of 2026,\" April 27, 2026, https://  rules.  house.gov/bill/119/hr-7567.")

On April 27, 2026, the House considered 57 amendments for H.R. 7567. Of the amendments made in order,

* 45 amendments passed by voice vote (24 as part of an en bloc amendment, 21 individually);
* 5 amendments passed by recorded vote;
* 4 amendments failed by recorded vote; and
* 3 amendments were not offered.

The House passed H.R. 7567, as amended, by a vote of 224-200 on April 30, 2026.

Senate Action

The Senate Committee on Agriculture, Nutrition, and Forestry chairman released a discussion draft of the Agriculture Act of 2026 on June 23, 2026. The committee posted updated versions on July 9 and July 31.[5](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R48918.html#fn5 "U.S. Senate Committee on Agriculture, Nutrition, and Forestry, \"Farm Bill 2.0,\" https://www.agriculture.senate.gov/  agricultural-act-of-2026-farm-bill-20 . See the discussion draft s of the Agriculture Act of 2026 at https://  www.  agriculture.  senate.gov/imo/media/doc/agricultural_act_of_2026.pdf (June 23), https://www.agriculture.senate.  gov/  imo/media/doc/agricultural_act_of_20261.pdf (July 9), and https://www.agriculture.senate.gov/agricultural-act-of-2026-farm-bill-2...") The committee marked up the discussion draft on August 6, 2026, by adding and disposing of amendments but did not have sufficient votes for final passage to report the bill (vote of 10-11).[6](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R48918.html#fn6 "U.S. Senate Committee on Agriculture, Nutrition, and Forestry, \" Business Meeting\" August 6, 2026, https://www.  agriculture.  senate.gov/hearings/business-meeting-08-06-2026 .") In committee markup,

* 34 amendments passed by voice vote as part of an en bloc amendment (including 2 amendments incorporated as secondary amendments);
* 1 amendment passed by recorded vote;
* 13 amendments failed by recorded vote; and
* 4 amendments were offered and withdrawn after discussion.

On September 16, 2026, the committee ordered the markup to be reported, as amended on August 6 and without further amendment, by a vote of 12-11.[7](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R48918.html#fn7 "U.S. Senate Committee on Agriculture, Nutrition, and Forestry, \" Business Meeting\" September 16, 2026, https://  www.  agriculture.senate.gov/hearings/business-meeting-09-16-2026 .") The bill, [S. 5526](http://www.congress.gov/bill/119/s/5526), was reported to the Senate on September 24, 2026.

This report provides a summary of each title included in the House-passed version of [H.R. 7567](http://www.congress.gov/cgi-lis/bdquery/z?d119:H.R.7567:) and the Senate committee-reported [S. 5526](http://www.congress.gov/bill/119/s/5526). Following the summary of each of the 12 titles included in [H.R. 7567](http://www.congress.gov/cgi-lis/bdquery/z?d119:H.R.7567:) and [S. 5526](http://www.congress.gov/bill/119/s/5526), this report includes tables describing each provision in the House-passed and Senate committee-reported bills and a comparison of the House-passed bill, Senate committee-reported bill, and current law. For any program authority affected by an extension, the most recent extension law is noted. In certain cases, the Senate committee-reported bill includes comparable provisions that are in a different title from the House-passed bill. In those cases, the provisions are cross-referenced in the title where the House-passed bill provision is located and the title where the Senate committee-reported bill provision is located.

Budgetary Impact

The Congressional Budget Office (CBO) released a score of [H.R. 7567](http://www.congress.gov/cgi-lis/bdquery/z?d119:H.R.7567:), as reported, on April 24, 2026, ahead of House floor consideration.[8](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R48918.html#fn8 "Congressional Budget Office (CBO) , H.R. 7567, Farm, Food, and National Security Act of 2026 , April 24, 2026, https://www.cbo.gov/publication/62376 .") CBO released a score of [S. 5526](http://www.congress.gov/bill/119/s/5526) on October 28, 2026.[9](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R48918.html#fn9 "CBO , Estimated Budgetary Effects of S. 5526, the Agricultural Act of 2026 , October 28, 2026, https://www.cbo.gov/publication/62881 .") As of this date, the budgetary impacts discussed below are for H.R. 7567.

The score of [H.R. 7567](http://www.congress.gov/cgi-lis/bdquery/z?d119:H.R.7567:) indicates that the bill would be budget neutral for mandatory (direct) spending over an 11-year budget window (FY2026-FY2036). In the shorter term, it is expected to increase mandatory spending by $162 million over the first six years (FY2026-FY2031) ([**Table 1**](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R48918.html#_Ref243994258)). Changes in the score are relative to the February 2026 CBO baseline ([**Table 2**](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R48918.html#_Ref180404519)).[10](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R48918.html#fn10 "CBO , \" Details About Baseline Projections for Selected Programs ,\" February 2026, https://www.cbo.gov/data/baseline-projections-selected-programs . ")

The largest budgetary changes to mandatory spending are in the conservation title; the bill would reduce outlays for the Environmental Quality Incentives Program (EQIP) by $786 million over FY2026-FY2036 and redistribute funding to other conservation programs, most with temporary budget effects. The bill would extend authority in the trade title to replenish the Bill Emerson Humanitarian Trust; its budget effects would be offset from restructuring trade promotion authorities that were included in the FY2025 budget reconciliation law ([P.L. 119-21](http://www.congress.gov/cgi-lis/bdquery/R?d119:FLD002:@1(119+21))). The bill would also extend funding in the energy title for the Biobased Markets Program, offset by a rescission to the Biorefinery Assistance Program.

For discretionary spending programs, CBO estimates that increases in authorizations that are subject to appropriation total $22 billion over 5 years (FY2027-FY2031) and $22 billion over 10 years (FY2027-FY2036) ([**Table 3**](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R48918.html#_Ref243994274)). FY2026 is not included in these estimates because appropriations have been enacted. Estimated outlays from these authorizations of appropriations are nearly $16 billion over 5 years (FY2027-FY2031) and $21 billion over 10 years (FY2027-FY2036). Details are not available about the shares that are reauthorizations of currently authorized appropriations and the amounts that are for new programming.

|  |
| --- |
| Budget Background for the Farm Bill  Budget enforcement in Congress for mandatory spending uses baseline and scoring procedures that are followed by the nonpartisan Congressional Budget Office (CBO). The goal is to determine whether proposed changes in a bill would increase or decrease government spending. The *baseline* is a projection of what outlays would be under current law if it were continued; it is the benchmark against which proposed changes in a bill are compared. The baseline incorporates current assumptions about economic conditions, including expectations about prices, acreage, trade, inflation, poverty, program participation, and eligibility. The *score* is the effect that each provision, or the bill in total, is expected to have compared with the baseline (CRS In Focus IF13124, [*Distinguishing Between Discretionary and Mandatory Spending*](http://www.crs.gov/Reports/IF13124), and CRS Report 98-560, [*Baselines and Scorekeeping in the Federal Budget Process*](http://www.crs.gov/Reports/98-560)).  The total score of a bill determines whether the bill meets budget enforcement requirements, such as pay-as-you-go (PAYGO) or cut-as-you-go (CUTGO) (CRS In Focus IF11032, [*Budgetary Decisionmaking in Congress*](http://www.crs.gov/Reports/IF11032)). A bill may add or subtract funds from programs or transfer funds among programs and titles using reductions to offset increases. *P**AYGO* refers to both a law and House and Senate rules that bills should not increase the deficit— essentially, that budgetary increases are fully offset by spending reductions or additional revenue, so that the net score of a bill is zero (CRS Report R41157, [*The Statutory Pay-As-You-Go Act of 2010: Summary and Legislative History*](http://www.crs.gov/Reports/R41157); CRS Report R47413, [*Points of Order in the Congressional Budget Process*](http://www.crs.gov/Reports/R47413); and CRS Report RL31943, [*Budget Enforcement Procedures: The Senate Pay-As-You-Go (PAYGO) Rule*](http://www.crs.gov/Reports/RL31943)). *C**UTGO* is a protocol in the House during the 119th Congress prohibiting offsets from having revenue-raising provisions (CRS Report R41510, [*House Rule XXI, Clause 10: The CUTGO Rule*](http://www.crs.gov/Reports/R41510)).  Budget laws require CBO to score proposed changes over an 11-year budget window regardless of the length of the new authorization period. The current 11-year scoring period is FY2026-FY2036 for authorizations in [H.R. 7567](http://www.congress.gov/cgi-lis/bdquery/z?d119:H.R.7567:) that generally would expire in FY2031 ([**Table 1**](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R48918.html#_Ref243994258)).  Four titles of the 2018 farm bill account for 99% of the baseline projection (nutrition, crop insurance, farm commodities, and conservation). The total 10-year baseline is $1.374 trillion over FY2027-FY2036 ([**Table 2**](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R48918.html#_Ref180404519)) (CRS In Focus IF12233, [*Farm Bill Primer: Budget Dynamics*](http://www.crs.gov/Reports/IF12233)). |

Title-by-Title Summary

Title I, Commodity Program[11](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R48918.html#fn11 "This section was prepared by Christine Whitt, CRS Analyst in Agricultural Policy, Resources, Science, and Industry Division (RSI); Jim Monke, CRS Specialist in Agricultural Policy, RSI; Stephanie Rosch, CRS Analyst in Agricultural Policy, RSI; and Megan Stubbs, CRS Specialist in Agricultural Conservation and Natural Resources, RSI.")

The respective commodity titles of [H.R. 7567](http://www.congress.gov/cgi-lis/bdquery/z?d119:H.R.7567:), as passed by the House, and of [S. 5526](http://www.congress.gov/bill/119/s/5526), as reported by the Senate Committee on Agriculture, Nutrition, and Forestry, would authorize and amend many of the agricultural commodity support and disaster assistance programs administered by the Farm Service Agency in USDA ([**Table 4**](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R48918.html#_Ref226733354)). Title I of the FY2025 budget reconciliation law ([P.L. 119-21](http://www.congress.gov/cgi-lis/bdquery/R?d119:FLD002:@1(119+21))) amended and/or reauthorized various programs included in the commodity title of the 2018 farm bill through the 2031 crop year.[12](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R48918.html#fn12 "See CRS Report R48574, One Big Beautiful Bill Act (H.R. 1): Title I, Farm Safety Net and Miscellaneous Provisions , coordinated by Stephanie Rosch .") As a result, [H.R. 7567](http://www.congress.gov/cgi-lis/bdquery/z?d119:H.R.7567:) and [S. 5526](http://www.congress.gov/bill/119/s/5526) do not include provisions relating to many of these programs, and the scope of the commodity title in the House-passed bill and in the Senate committee-reported bill is more limited than in previous farm bills.

Commodity Policy

[H.R. 7567](http://www.congress.gov/cgi-lis/bdquery/z?d119:H.R.7567:) and [S. 5526](http://www.congress.gov/bill/119/s/5526) would continue the suspension of non-expiring farm bill commodity support provisions from the 1930s and 1940s through crop year 2031, as was done in recent farm bills.[13](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R48918.html#fn13 "See CRS Report R47659, Expiration of the 2018 Farm Bill and Extension for 2025 , by Jim Monke, Randy Alison Aussenberg, and Megan Stubbs .") [H.R. 7567](http://www.congress.gov/bill/119/hr/7567) would not make changes to the commodities eligible for support from the Agriculture Risk Coverage (ARC), Price Loss Coverage (PLC), or Marketing Assistance Loan (MAL) programs.[14](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R48918.html#fn14 "For background on the Agriculture Risk Coverage, Price Loss Coverage, and Marketing Assistance Loan programs, see CRS Report R45730, Farm Commodity Provisions in the 2018 Farm Bill (P.L. 115-334) , by Stephanie Rosch .") [S. 5526](http://www.congress.gov/bill/119/s/5526) would require the Secretary of Agriculture to study making dry edible beans eligible for these programs and authorize appropriations of "such sums as necessary" for a new program to reduce and maintain dry edible beans stocks-to-use ratios at historical levels. [H.R. 7567](http://www.congress.gov/bill/119/hr/7567) would not make changes to the eligible entities who can submit information to USDA about a producer's average adjusted gross income.[15](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R48918.html#fn15 "Statute restricts eligibility for certain farm programs based on a producer's average adjusted gross income. For background, see CRS Report R46248, U.S. Farm Programs: Eligibility and Payment Limits , by Megan Stubbs and Stephanie Rosch .") [S. 5526](http://www.congress.gov/bill/119/s/5526) would add enrolled agents licensed to provide tax services by the U.S. Treasury to the list of eligible entities. [H.R. 7567](http://www.congress.gov/bill/119/hr/7567) would not make changes to farmers' *base acres*.[16](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R48918.html#fn16 "For background on base acres, see CRS In Focus IF12418, U.S. Farm Commodity Support: Base Acres and Payment Yields , by Stephanie Rosch and Christine Whitt .") [S. 5526](http://www.congress.gov/bill/119/s/5526) would require the Secretary to grant up to an additional 1.5 million base acres to owners of eligible farms.[17](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R48918.html#fn17 "Congress previously required the Secretary of Agriculture to grant up to 30 million additional base acres to owners of eligible farms in the FY2025 budget reconciliation law. For background, see CRS Report R48574, One Big Beautiful Bill Act (H.R. 1): Title I, Farm Safety Net and Miscellaneous Provisions , coordinated by Stephanie Rosch . The Senate committee-reported bill would amend the provision that authorized the 30 million additional base acres to be 31.5 million base acres, thereby r...")

[H.R. 7567](http://www.congress.gov/cgi-lis/bdquery/z?d119:H.R.7567:) and [S. 5526](http://www.congress.gov/bill/119/s/5526) would mandate that dairy product manufacturers report production costs and yield information to USDA. This information would be used to update factors that represent the costs to manufacture a dairy product (the dairy industry refers to these costs as *make* *allowances*) for the Federal Milk Marketing Order (FMMO) system.[18](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R48918.html#fn18 "See CRS In Focus IF12923, Pricing Amendments to the Federal Milk Marketing Orders , by Christine Whitt ; and CRS Report R48573, U.S. Dairy Policy , by Christine Whitt .") Both bills would clarify the timeline for USDA to submit certain dairy reports to the House Committee on Agriculture and the Senate Committee on Agriculture, Nutrition, and Forestry. Both bills would make the Dairy Forward Pricing Program permanent.[19](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R48918.html#fn19 "See CRS Report R48573, U.S. Dairy Policy , by Christine Whitt .") [H.R. 7567](http://www.congress.gov/cgi-lis/bdquery/z?d119:H.R.7567:) would increase the number of eligible entities for the Dairy Business Innovation Initiatives, whereas the Senate committee-reported bill would increase the program's authorized appropriations. [S. 5526](http://www.congress.gov/bill/119/s/5526) would make the Dairy Margin Coverage (DMC) program free for participating dairy producers enrolling six million pounds or less of their historical milk production in the program.

[H.R. 7567](http://www.congress.gov/cgi-lis/bdquery/z?d119:H.R.7567:) and [S. 5526](http://www.congress.gov/bill/119/s/5526) would allow producers to repay nonrecourse marketing assistance loans during a lapse in appropriations (i.e., during a government shutdown) when USDA employees may be furloughed.[20](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R48918.html#fn20 "See CRS In Focus IF12140, Farm Bill Primer: MAL and LDP Farm Support Programs , by Stephanie Rosch .") [H.R. 7567](http://www.congress.gov/bill/119/hr/7567) would authorize USDA to provide storage facility loans for on-farm storage of propane used for agricultural production and to conduct a study on the feasibility of providing storage facility loans for fertilizer. [S. 5526](http://www.congress.gov/bill/119/s/5526) would authorize storage facility loans for on-farm storage of propane and fertilizer, including equipment and infrastructure necessary for fertilizer storage.

The Secretary of Agriculture has broad authority to use Commodity Credit Corporation (CCC) funding to support agricultural commodities.[21](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R48918.html#fn21 "See CRS Report R44606, The Commodity Credit Corporation (CCC) , by Megan Stubbs .") [H.R. 7567](http://www.congress.gov/cgi-lis/bdquery/z?d119:H.R.7567:) would remove the exclusion for tobacco from the list of eligible agricultural commodities, thereby restoring tobacco eligibility for funding from the CCC.[22](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R48918.html#fn22 "See CRS In Focus IF13196, Farm Support for Tobacco and the 2026 Farm Bill , by Stephanie Rosch .") [S. 5526](http://www.congress.gov/bill/119/s/5526) would make no changes to the exclusion of tobacco from the list of CCC-eligible agricultural commodities (i.e., the bill would retain the existing statutory exclusion for tobacco). [S. 5526](http://www.congress.gov/bill/119/s/5526) would also amend and add reporting requirements of CCC expenditures and activities.

Agricultural Disaster Assistance Programs

[H.R. 7567](http://www.congress.gov/cgi-lis/bdquery/z?d119:H.R.7567:) and [S. 5526](http://www.congress.gov/bill/119/s/5526) would amend the Tree Assistance Program (TAP) to provide payment recipients flexibility in replanting after losses and give recipients the option of receiving an initial partial payment prior to incurring replanting or rehabilitation costs. In [H.R. 7567](http://www.congress.gov/cgi-lis/bdquery/z?d119:H.R.7567:) and [S. 5526](http://www.congress.gov/bill/119/s/5526), USDA's authority to offer initial partial payments would expire (sunset) on September 30, 2035. [S. 5526](http://www.congress.gov/bill/119/s/5526) includes provisions that would expand covered losses under TAP to commercial trees that are no longer commercially viable due to a natural disaster. [H.R. 7567](http://www.congress.gov/cgi-lis/bdquery/z?d119:H.R.7567:) and [S. 5526](http://www.congress.gov/bill/119/s/5526) would require USDA to establish a framework to provide assistance to specialty crop producers for certain losses, including economic crises and market disruptions. [S. 5526](http://www.congress.gov/bill/119/s/5526) includes a provision that defines the term *specialty crop*. [H.R. 7567](http://www.congress.gov/cgi-lis/bdquery/z?d119:H.R.7567:) and [S. 5526](http://www.congress.gov/bill/119/s/5526) would authorize USDA to use block grants for administering supplemental ad hoc agricultural disaster assistance. [S. 5526](http://www.congress.gov/bill/119/s/5526) includes language clarifying that USDA is required to administer such block grants via states. [H.R. 7567](http://www.congress.gov/cgi-lis/bdquery/z?d119:H.R.7567:) would require USDA to expand the proof of death standards in the Livestock Indemnity Program (LIP) for losses due to depredation by Mexican wolves.[23](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R48918.html#fn23 "See CRS In Focus IF12101, Farm Bill Primer: Disaster Assistance , by Christine Whitt .") [S. 5526](http://www.congress.gov/bill/119/s/5526) would clarify that livestock losses due to depredation by Mexican gray wolves and panthers are eligible under LIP. In addition, [S. 5526](http://www.congress.gov/bill/119/s/5526) would require USDA to accept documentation showing probability or confirmation of an eligible livestock attack by animals or avian predators.

[S. 5526](http://www.congress.gov/bill/119/s/5526) would clarify that the definition of livestock used to determine eligibility for USDA's livestock disaster assistance program includes unweaned livestock. The Senate committee-reported bill would also expand covered losses under the Noninsured Crop Disaster Assistance Program (NAP). In addition, [S. 5526](http://www.congress.gov/bill/119/s/5526) would codify elements of LIP and the Emergency Assistance for Livestock, Honeybees, and Farm-Raised Fish that can be found in the programs' respective regulations.

| **Suspension of permanent price support authority****.** Suspends the permanent price support authority of the Agricultural Adjustment Act of 1938 (P.L. 75-430) and the Agricultural Adjustment Act of 1949 (P.L. 89-439) for certain commodities for the 2014-2026 crop years and for milk through December 31, 2026. **(7 U.S.C. §9092; P.L. 11****9****-****37****)** | **Suspension of permanent price support authority.** Extends the suspension of permanent price authority through crop year 2031 for commodities other than dairy. Extends the suspension for dairy through December 31, 2031. **(§1****001****)** | **Suspension of permanent price support authority**. Identical to the House provision. **(§1****101)** |
| **Tree Assistance Program (TAP).** Provides payments to eligible orchardists and nursery growers to replant or rehabilitate trees, bushes, and vines damaged by natural disasters. Eligible losses must exceed normal mortality. Payments reimburse eligible orchardists and nursery growers for 65% of the cost of replanting trees or nursery stock and 50% of the cost of rehabilitation (e.g., pruning and removal). **(****7 U.S.C. §9081(e)****)** | **Tree assistance** **p****rogram.**Expands coverage to include biennial tree crops and losses due to pest infestations. Specifies that trees no longer producing an economically viable crop as a result of a natural disaster are eligible for TAP payments. Adds requirements for TAP recipients to replant or rehabilitate trees within two years after the application approval or when necessary to ensure tree survival. Provides recipients flexibilities in alternative planting activities that can be reimbursed, including replanting of alternative varieties and alternative stand densities and in alternative locations. Additional payments are not provided for these alternative activities. Requires USDA to notify applicants of application receipt and approve or deny the application within 120 days of submission. Adds authority for USDA to administer an initial payment before incurring eligible covered costs. Adds required payment calculation components, such as estimates for initial partial payments for the cost of replanting or rehabilitating the eligible tree, bush, or vine; subsequent payments; and potential overpayments. The payments provisions sunset in September 2035. **(****§1002)** | **Tree assistance** **p****rogram.** Expands the program, timing requirements, flexibilities and payment limitations for alternatives used in replanting. The requirement to notify applicants within 120 days, initial payments, and sunset provisions are functionally the same as the House provision. Does not include the economically viable provision. **(§1303)** |
| No comparable provision. | **Specialty** **c****rop** **e****mergency** **a****ssistance** **f****ramework.** Requires USDA to establish a framework to provide payments to specialty crop producers impacted by adverse events, such as economic crises and market disruptions. Requires USDA to calculate payments on the basis of the producer's previous sales history and availability of funds. Authorizes USDA to create special rules that take into account crop value, production costs, and the legal and organizational structure of producers. Applies payment limits used for other USDA direct payment programs and excepts entities that derive 75% of their average gross income from farming and other related activities. Authorizes USDA to establish a separate payment limit of not less than $900,000 for each excepted entity for any crop year. Applies producer reporting and payment limits used in other USDA direct payment programs. Does not specify a funding mechanism for this framework. **(****§1003)** | **Specialty** **c****rop** **e****mergency** **a****ssistance** **f****ramework.**Defines a *specialty crop* to mean the same collection of crops defined in 7 U.S.C. §1621 note. This definition includes fruits and vegetables, tree nuts, dried fruits, and horticulture and nursery crops (including floriculture). Other provisions are functionally the same as the House provision. **(****§1304)** |
| No comparable provision. | **Assistance in the form of block grants.** Authorizes USDA to use block grants when administering additional funds for agricultural disaster assistance to address losses for which other federal assistance is unavailable. **(§1004)** | **Assistance in the form of block grants.** Provides the same general block grant authority as H.R. 7567. Specifies that USDA may make such block grants to states and that timber is an eligible commodity. **(§1305)** |
| **Dairy Forward Pricing Program.** Authorizes a USDA dairy forward pricing program that applies to milk purchased for manufactured products and excludes milk purchased for fluid consumption. Expires September 30, 2026. **(7 U.S.C. §8772; P.L. 11****9****-****37****)** | **Dairy-related extension****s****.** Removes the program termination date.**(§****1005****(a))** | **Reauthorizations.** Contains minor wording and grammatical differences from the House provision. **(****§****1****201(a))** |
| **Dairy Indemnity Program.** Authorizes payments to dairy farmers when a public regulatory agency directs removal of raw milk from the market due to pesticide contamination, nuclear radiation or fallout, or toxic substances and other chemical residues. Authority expires September 30, 2026. **(7 U.S.C. §4553; P.L.** **119-37****)** | **Dairy-related extension****s****.** Extends authority through September 30, 2031. **(§****1005****(b))** | **Reauthorizations.** Contains minor wording and grammatical differences from the House provision. **(****§****1****201(b))** |
| **Dairy Promotion and Research Program.** Authorizes the National Dairy Promotion and Research Board to oversee a generic dairy product promotion and a research and nutrition education program (i.e., "dairy checkoff") and to spend funds to develop foreign markets for U.S. dairy products. Authority expires September 30, 2026. **(7 U.S.C. §4504(e)(2); P.L. 11****9-37****)** | **Dairy-related extension****s****.** Extends authority through September 30, 2031. **(§****1005****(c))** | **Reauthorizations.** Contains minor wording and grammatical differences from the House provision. **(****§****1****201(b))** |
| **Mandatory reporting for dairy products.** Requires USDA to establish a mandatory program for dairy product manufacturers to report to USDA price, quantity, and moisture content of sold products. **(7 U.S.C. §1637b)** | **Mandatory reporting of dairy product processing costs.**Amends the manufacturers reporting requirements to include production costs and product yield information to USDA as determined by the Secretary of Agriculture. **Note:**

a. "Agriculture committees of jurisdiction" refers to the House Committee on Agriculture and the Senate Committee on Agriculture, Nutrition, and Forestry.

Title II, Conservation[24](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R48918.html#fn24 "This section was prepared by Megan Stubbs, CRS Specialist in Agricultural Conservation and Natural Resources , R SI .")

The respective conservation titles of [H.R. 7567](http://www.congress.gov/cgi-lis/bdquery/z?d119:H.R.7567:), as passed by the House, and of [S. 5526](http://www.congress.gov/bill/119/s/5526), as reported by the Senate Committee on Agriculture, Nutrition, and Forestry, propose reauthorizations, amendments, and new programs that aim to incentivize farmers and ranchers to voluntarily implement resource-conserving practices on private land. Both bills would reauthorize expiring programs and provisions, create a new forest easement program and a new state-centered soil health program, and emphasize the goal of streamlining conservation program delivery ([**Table 5**](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R48918.html#_Ref166862979)). The proposed changes in [H.R. 7567](http://www.congress.gov/cgi-lis/bdquery/z?d119:H.R.7567:) center on expanding precision agriculture, establishing wildlife corridor habitat, and amending program implementation requirements. The proposed changes in [S. 5526](http://www.congress.gov/bill/119/s/5526) focus on drought and water-related activities, amendment of existing emergency and watershed programs, and guidelines for conservation practice standards.

Conservation Reserve Program

The Conservation Reserve Program (CRP) provides federal payments to landowners to remove agricultural land from production and restricts the conversion of grasslands to non-grazing uses. Under both [H.R. 7567](http://www.congress.gov/cgi-lis/bdquery/z?d119:H.R.7567:) and [S. 5526](http://www.congress.gov/bill/119/s/5526), CRP would be reauthorized at its current level of 27 million acres through FY2031. Existing CRP subprograms would be reauthorized at current levels, including the Conservation Reserve Enhancement Program (8.6 million acres of total CRP acres), CRP grassland contracts (a minimum of 2 million acres of total CRP acres), and the Farmable Wetlands Program (not more than 750,000 acres total).

Under [H.R. 7567](http://www.congress.gov/cgi-lis/bdquery/z?d119:H.R.7567:), funding for CRP initiatives would be reauthorized, including $12 million total for forest management incentive payments and $50 million total for the Transition Incentives Program. [S. 5526](http://www.congress.gov/bill/119/s/5526) would not reauthorize these initiatives.

The Senate committee-reported bill would limit enrollment in CRP grassland contracts (maximum of 12 million acres of total CRP) and expand payments for grazing and water infrastructure. [S. 5526](http://www.congress.gov/bill/119/s/5526) would also provide additional flexibilities for haying during the last two weeks of the primary nesting season if the activity would not cause long-term damage to the vegetative cover for wildlife populations. Additionally, [S. 5526](http://www.congress.gov/bill/119/s/5526) would increase the rental payment limit of $50,000 per fiscal year to $125,000 per fiscal year, the first increase since the program's creation in 1985.[25](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R48918.html#fn25 "The Conservation Reserve Program (CRP) was originally established in the Food Security Act of 1985 (P.L. 99-198).")

Environmental Quality Incentives Program and Conservation Stewardship Program

The two working lands programs, the Environmental Quality Incentives Program (EQIP) and the Conservation Stewardship Program (CSP), provide technical and financial assistance to farmers to improve land management practices. Many of the proposed amendments in [H.R. 7567](http://www.congress.gov/cgi-lis/bdquery/z?d119:H.R.7567:) to EQIP and CSP would emphasize the use of precision agriculture practices and technology, composting, and wildlife corridor habitat. The bill would create new subprograms and initiatives under both programs, including a U.S. southern border initiative under EQIP and a state soil health initiative under CSP. Funding for the new initiatives would come from existing funds authorized for EQIP and CSP. Payment limits restricting total funds received per person under EQIP and CSP, which have expired, would be reestablished and in effect through FY2031.[26](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R48918.html#fn26 "Payment limits under the Environmental Quality Incentives Program (EQIP) and Conservation Stewardship Program (CSP) restrict the amount of funds a person or legal entity may receive under the program. Limits under both programs expired in FY2023 and were extended through FY2024 under the FY2024 farm bill extension ( P.L. 118-22 , Division B, §102). The most recent two farm bill extensions have excluded EQIP and CSP payments limits ( P.L. 118-158 , Division D, §4101(e)(2)(B); and P.L. 119-37 ,...") [H.R. 7567](http://www.congress.gov/cgi-lis/bdquery/z?d119:H.R.7567:) would use EQIP funding to pay for a new Forest Conservation Easement Program (FCEP) and funding increases in other conservation programs. In total, [H.R. 7567](http://www.congress.gov/cgi-lis/bdquery/z?d119:H.R.7567:) is estimated to reduce EQIP budget authority by $1.0 billion over 10 years (FY2026-2036), less sequestration. This is estimated to result in $786 million less in EQIP spending (outlays) over the same period, less sequestration.[27](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R48918.html#fn27 "See Table 3 in C BO , Estimated Changes in Direct Spending Under H.R. 7567, the Farm, Food, and National Security Act of 2026 , February 23, 2026, pp. 4-5, https://www.cbo.gov/system/files/2026-02/hr7567.pdf .")

The Senate committee-reported bill and the House-passed bill contain similar language relating to precision agriculture, but [S. 5526](http://www.congress.gov/bill/119/s/5526) does not include composting in its definition of the term. Payment limits for both EQIP and CSP would also be extended by [S. 5526](http://www.congress.gov/bill/119/s/5526). The soil health program created in the House-passed bill under CSP is created as a stand-alone program in the Senate committee-reported bill and referred to as a "conservation assistance program." The new program would authorize $50 million annually through FY2031 and be offset, in part, with the bill's reductions to EQIP and CSP.

Agricultural Conservation Easement Program

The Agricultural Conservation Easement Program (ACEP) provides financial and technical assistance through two types of easements: (1) agricultural land easements that limit nonagricultural uses on productive farmland or grassland and (2) wetland reserve easements that protect and restore wetlands. Most of the proposed changes to ACEP in [H.R. 7567](http://www.congress.gov/cgi-lis/bdquery/z?d119:H.R.7567:) focus on additional incentives for socially disadvantaged farmer participation, the federal share of easement costs, enforcement rights of an easement, and modification and exchange requirements. The bill would exempt ACEP participants from the adjusted gross income (AGI) limit, which restricts eligibility for various USDA programs to persons and legal entities whose average AGI is less than $900,000, unless 75% or more of the income is from farming, ranching, or silviculture activities.[28](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R48918.html#fn28 "7 U.S.C. §1308-3a.")

[S. 5526](http://www.congress.gov/bill/119/s/5526) would also include the AGI exemption for ACEP participants but with different changes to the federal share of easement costs, certification of eligible entities, and the use of de minimis adjustments to easements.

Forest Conservation Easement Program

The House-passed and Senate committee-reported bills would both create a new Forest Conservation Easement Program (FCEP) that would fund two types of easements: forest land easements and forest reserve easements. Forest land easements would be similar to ACEP agricultural land easements in that they would protect the sustainability of forest lands by limiting non-forest land uses. Forest reserve easements would be similar to Healthy Forests Reserve Program (HFRP) easements in that they would protect and enhance forest ecosystems and species habitat. Both bills would repeal HFRP and provide FCEP mandatory funding through FY2031.

Other Conservation Programs

Both [H.R. 7567](http://www.congress.gov/cgi-lis/bdquery/z?d119:H.R.7567:) and [S. 5526](http://www.congress.gov/bill/119/s/5526) include adjustments to other conservation programs. The House-passed bill would increase funding for programs such as the Feral Swine Eradication and Control Program (FSCP) and make changes relating to the delivery of technical assistance, streamlining, adjustment of federal cost share, or altering of eligibility requirements to programs (e.g., the Regional Conservation Partnership Program, Emergency Conservation Program (ECP), Emergency Watershed Protection Program, and Watershed Rehabilitation Program).

The Senate committee-reported bill includes some of the House-passed bill's proposed changes, such as the funding increases to FSCP, changes relating to the delivery of technical assistance, and updated technology options under ECP. Amendments to the Emergency Forest Restoration Program and Watershed Protection and Flood Prevention Act (P.L. 83-566) are included in the Senate committee-reported bill and not in the House-passed bill.

Nearly all of the conservation programs receive mandatory funding. Much of this funding was adjusted under the FY2025 budget reconciliation law.[29](https://www.congress.gov/crs_external_products/R/HTML/R48918.html#fn29 "For additional information, see CRS In Focus IF13114, Agricultural Conservation After Enactment of the FY2025 Budget Reconciliation Law (P.L. 119-21) , by Megan Stubbs .") Under [H.R. 7567](http://www.congress.gov/cgi-lis/bdquery/z?d119:H.R.7567:) and [S. 5526](http://www.congress.gov/bill/119/s/5526), the conservation title is estimated to be budget neutral with reductions in EQIP and CSP offsetting increases in other programs.
